					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Archbishop of Canterbury from 1533 to 1555</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">


</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color: #DFB0DF;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;display:inline;"><span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/The_Right_Reverend" title="The Right Reverend">The Right Reverend</a>&#160;and <a href="/wiki/The_Right_Honourable" title="The Right Honourable">Right Honourable</a></span></div><br /><div style="display:inline" class="fn">Thomas Cranmer</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-subheader role"><a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Cranmer_by_Gerlach_Flicke.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Thomas_Cranmer_by_Gerlach_Flicke.jpg/220px-Thomas_Cranmer_by_Gerlach_Flicke.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Thomas_Cranmer_by_Gerlach_Flicke.jpg/330px-Thomas_Cranmer_by_Gerlach_Flicke.jpg 1.5x, class="infobox-caption">Portrait, 1545<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Church</th><td class="infobox-data org"><a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Diocese</th><td class="infobox-data December 1533<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="note"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Term ended</th><td class="infobox-data">4 December 1555</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Predecessor</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/William_Warham" title="William Warham">William Warham</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Successor</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Reginald_Pole" title="Reginald Pole">Reginald Pole</a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:#DDDDDD;">Orders</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Consecration</th><td class="infobox-data">30 March 1533<br />by&#160;<a href="/wiki/John_Longland" title="John Longland">John Longland</a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:#DDDDDD;">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data">2 July 1489<br /><div style="display:inline" class="birthplace"><a href="/wiki/Aslockton" title="Aslockton">Aslockton</a>, Nottinghamshire, England</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">21 March 1556 (aged 66)<br /><a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Denomination</th><td class="infobox-data category"><a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a> (<a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Profession</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Priest_(Anglicanism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Priest (Anglicanism)">Priest</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma mater</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Jesus_College,_Cambridge" title="Jesus College, Cambridge">Jesus College, Cambridge</a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:#DDDDDD;">Sainthood</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Thomas Cranmer</b> (2 July 1489 – 21 March 1556) was a leader of the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">English Reformation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a> during the reigns of <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII" title="Henry VIII">Henry VIII</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_VI" title="Edward VI">Edward VI</a> and, for a short time, <a href="/wiki/Mary_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary I">Mary I</a>. He helped build the case for the annulment of Henry's marriage to <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Aragon" title="Catherine of Aragon">Catherine of Aragon</a>, which was one of the causes of the separation of the English Church from union with the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>. Along with <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cromwell" title="Thomas Cromwell">Thomas Cromwell</a>, he supported the principle of <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Supremacy" title="Acts of Supremacy">royal supremacy</a>, in which the king was considered sovereign over the Church within his realm.
</p><p>During Cranmer's tenure as Archbishop of Canterbury, he was responsible for establishing the first doctrinal and liturgical structures of the reformed <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>. Under Henry's rule, Cranmer did not make many radical changes in the Church, due to power struggles between religious conservatives and reformers. He published the first officially authorised <a href="/wiki/Vernacular" title="Vernacular">vernacular</a> service, the <i><a href="/wiki/Exhortation_and_Litany" title="Exhortation and Litany">Exhortation and Litany</a></i>.
</p><p>When Edward came to the throne, Cranmer was able to promote major reforms. He wrote and compiled the first two editions of the <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer" title="Book of Common Prayer">Book of Common Prayer</a></i>, a complete liturgy for the English Church. With the assistance of several Continental reformers to whom he gave refuge, he changed doctrine or discipline in areas such as the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clerical_celibacy" title="Clerical celibacy">clerical celibacy</a>, the role of <a href="/wiki/Religious_image" title="Religious image">images</a> in places of worship, and the <a href="/wiki/Veneration" title="Veneration">veneration</a> of saints. Cranmer <a href="/wiki/Promulgate" class="mw-redirect" title="Promulgate">promulgated</a> the new doctrines through the Prayer Book, the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Books_of_Homilies" title="The Books of Homilies">Homilies</a></i> and other publications.
</p><p>After the accession of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic">Catholic</a> Mary I, Cranmer was put on trial for treason and <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresy</a>. Imprisoned for over two years and under pressure from Church authorities, he made several <a href="/wiki/Recantation" title="Recantation">recantations</a> and apparently reconciled himself with the Catholic Church. While this would have normally absolved him, Mary wanted him executed, and he was <a href="/wiki/Death_by_burning" title="Death by burning">burned at the stake</a> on 21 March 1556; on the day of his execution, he withdrew his recantations, to die a <a href="/wiki/Heretic" class="mw-redirect" title="Heretic">heretic</a> to Catholics and a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> for the principles of the English Reformation. Cranmer's death was immortalised in <i><a href="/wiki/Foxe%27s_Book_of_Martyrs" title="Foxe&#39;s Book of Martyrs">Foxe's Book of Martyrs</a></i> and his legacy lives on within the Church of England through the <i>Book of Common Prayer</i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Thirty-Nine_Articles" class="mw-redirect" title="Thirty-Nine Articles">Thirty-Nine Articles</a></i>, an <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> statement of faith derived from his work.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins">Origins</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Origins">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cranmer_PaternalArms.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Cranmer_PaternalArms.png/170px-Cranmer_PaternalArms.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="195" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Cranmer_PaternalArms.png/255px-Cranmer_PaternalArms.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Cranmer_PaternalArms.png/340px-Cranmer_PaternalArms.png 2x" data-file-width="923" data-file-height="1061" /></a><figcaption>Cranmer's paternal <a href="/wiki/Canting_arms" title="Canting arms">canting arms</a>: <i>Argent, a chevron between three cranes azure</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrype1840181_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrype1840181-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Cranmer was born in 1489 at <a href="/wiki/Aslockton" title="Aslockton">Aslockton</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nottinghamshire" title="Nottinghamshire">Nottinghamshire</a>, England.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He was a younger son of Thomas Cranmer by his wife Agnes Hatfield. Thomas Cranmer was of modest wealth but was from a well-established <a href="/wiki/Armigerous" class="mw-redirect" title="Armigerous">armigerous</a> gentry family which took its name from the <a href="/wiki/Manorialism" title="Manorialism">manor</a> of <a href="/wiki/Cranmer_Hall,_Lincolnshire" title="Cranmer Hall, Lincolnshire">Cranmer</a> in Lincolnshire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHirst19342_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirst19342-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/Ledger_stone" title="Ledger stone">ledger stone</a> to one of his relatives in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St_John_of_Beverley,_Whatton" title="Church of St John of Beverley, Whatton">Church of St John of Beverley, Whatton</a>, near Aslockton is inscribed as follows: <i>Hic jacet Thomas Cranmer, Armiger, qui obiit vicesimo septimo die mensis Maii, anno d(omi)ni. MD centesimo primo, cui(us) a(n)i(ma)e p(ro)p(i)cietur Deus Amen</i> ("here lies Thomas Cranmer, Esquire, who died on the 27th day of May in the year of our lord 1501, on whose soul may God look upon with mercy"). The arms of the Cranmer and Aslockton families are displayed. The figure is that of a man in flowing hair and gown, and a purse at his right side.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown189181_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown189181-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Their oldest son, John Cranmer, inherited the family estate, whereas Thomas and his younger brother Edmund were placed on the path to a clerical career.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_years_.281489.E2.80.931527.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_(1489–1527)">Early years (1489–1527)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early years (1489–1527)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Historians know nothing definite about Cranmer's early schooling. He probably attended a grammar school in his village. At the age of 14, two years after the death of his father, he was sent to the newly created <a href="/wiki/Jesus_College,_Cambridge" title="Jesus College, Cambridge">Jesus College, Cambridge</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> It took him eight years to attain his Bachelor of Arts degree, following a curriculum of logic, classical literature and philosophy. During this time, he began to collect medieval <a href="/wiki/Scholasticism" title="Scholasticism">scholastic</a> books, which he preserved faithfully throughout his life.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> For his master's degree he studied the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance_humanism" title="Renaissance humanism">humanists</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Lef%C3%A8vre_d%27%C3%89taples" title="Jacques Lefèvre d&#39;Étaples">Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples</a> and <a href="/wiki/Erasmus" title="Erasmus">Erasmus</a>. He finished the course in three years.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after receiving his <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts_(Oxbridge_and_Dublin)" class="mw-redirect" title="Master of Arts (Oxbridge and Dublin)">Master of Arts</a> degree in 1515, he was elected to a <a href="/wiki/Fellow" title="Fellow">Fellowship</a> of Jesus College.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sometime after Cranmer took his MA, he married a woman named Joan. Although he was not yet a priest, he was obliged to give up his fellowship, resulting in the loss of his residence at Jesus College. To support himself and his wife, he took a job as a <a href="/wiki/Reader_(academic_rank)" title="Reader (academic rank)">reader</a> at <a href="/wiki/Buckingham_College,_Cambridge" title="Buckingham College, Cambridge">Buckingham Hall</a> (later reformed as <a href="/wiki/Magdalene_College,_Cambridge" title="Magdalene College, Cambridge">Magdalene College</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>  When Joan died during her first childbirth, Jesus College showed its regard for Cranmer by reinstating his fellowship. He began studying theology and by 1520 he had been <a href="/wiki/Holy_orders" title="Holy orders">ordained</a>, the university already having named him as one of its preachers. He received his <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Divinity" title="Doctor of Divinity">Doctor of Divinity</a> degree in 1526.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Not much is known about Cranmer's thoughts and experiences during his three decades at Cambridge. Traditionally, he has been portrayed as a humanist whose enthusiasm for biblical scholarship prepared him for the adoption of <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a> ideas, which were spreading during the 1520s. A study of his <a href="/wiki/Marginalia" title="Marginalia">marginalia</a> reveals an early antipathy to <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a> and an admiration for Erasmus.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> When <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Wolsey" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Wolsey">Cardinal Wolsey</a>, the king's <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a>, selected several Cambridge scholars, including <a href="/wiki/Edward_Lee,_Archbishop_of_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward Lee, Archbishop of York">Edward Lee</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Gardiner" title="Stephen Gardiner">Stephen Gardiner</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Sampson" title="Richard Sampson">Richard Sampson</a>, to be diplomats throughout Europe, Cranmer was chosen for an embassy to the Holy Roman Emperor. His supposed participation in an earlier embassy to Spain, mentioned in the older literature, has proved to be spurious.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="In_the_service_of_Henry_VIII_.281527.E2.80.931532.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_the_service_of_Henry_VIII_(1527–1532)">In the service of Henry VIII (1527–1532)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: In the service of Henry VIII (1527–1532)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hans_Holbein,_the_Younger,_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/170px-Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/255px-Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/340px-Hans_Holbein%2C_the_Younger%2C_Around_1497-1543_-_Portrait_of_Henry_VIII_of_England_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2994" data-file-height="4203" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII</a> recognised Cranmer's value in obtaining support for the annulment of his marriage with Catherine of Aragon. Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Hans_Holbein_the_Younger" title="Hans Holbein the Younger">Hans Holbein the Younger</a>, c. 1536</figcaption></figure>
<p>Henry VIII's first marriage had its origins in 1502 when his elder brother, <a href="/wiki/Arthur,_Prince_of_Wales" title="Arthur, Prince of Wales">Arthur</a>, died. Their father, <a href="/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England" title="Henry VII of England">Henry VII</a>, then betrothed Arthur's widow, Catherine of Aragon, to the future king. The betrothal immediately raised questions related to the biblical prohibition (in Leviticus 18 and 20) against marriage to a brother's wife. The couple married in 1509 and after a series of miscarriages, a daughter, <a href="/wiki/Mary_I_of_England" title="Mary I of England">Mary</a>, was born in 1516. By the 1520s, Henry still did not have a son to name as heir and he took this as a sure sign of God's anger and made overtures to the Vatican about an <a href="/wiki/Annulment" title="Annulment">annulment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He gave Cardinal Wolsey the task of prosecuting his case; Wolsey began by consulting university experts. From 1527, in addition to his duties as a Cambridge don, Cranmer assisted with the annulment proceedings.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In mid-1529, Cranmer stayed with relatives in <a href="/wiki/Waltham_Holy_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Waltham Holy Cross">Waltham Holy Cross</a> to avoid an outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a> in Cambridge. Two of his Cambridge associates, Stephen Gardiner and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Foxe" title="Edward Foxe">Edward Foxe</a>, joined him. The three discussed the annulment issue and Cranmer suggested putting aside the legal case in Rome in favour of a general canvassing of opinions from university theologians throughout Europe. Henry showed much interest in the idea when Gardiner and Foxe presented him this plan. It is not known whether the king or his new Lord Chancellor, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_More" title="Thomas More">Thomas More</a>, explicitly approved the plan. Eventually it was implemented and Cranmer was requested to join the royal team in Rome to gather opinions from the universities.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Edward Foxe coordinated the research effort and the team produced the <i><a href="/wiki/Collectanea_satis_copiosa" title="Collectanea satis copiosa">Collectanea Satis Copiosa</a></i> ("The Sufficiently Abundant Collections") and <i>The Determinations</i>, historical and theological support for the argument that the king exercised supreme jurisdiction within his realm.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cranmer_BishopricArms.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cranmer_BishopricArms.png/200px-Cranmer_BishopricArms.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cranmer_BishopricArms.png/300px-Cranmer_BishopricArms.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cranmer_BishopricArms.png/400px-Cranmer_BishopricArms.png 2x" data-file-width="923" data-file-height="1062" /></a><figcaption>New arms granted <i>circa</i> 1544 to Thomas Cranmer by King Henry VIII, in lieu of his paternal arms: <i>Argent, on a chevron azure between three <a href="/wiki/Pelican_in_her_piety" class="mw-redirect" title="Pelican in her piety">pelicans</a> sable <a href="/wiki/Pelican_in_her_piety" class="mw-redirect" title="Pelican in her piety">vulning themselves</a> <a href="/wiki/Tincture_(heraldry)" title="Tincture (heraldry)">proper</a> as many cinquefoils or</i>, telling him "That those birds should signify unto him, that he ought to be ready, as the pelican is, to shed his blood for his young ones, brought up in the faith of Christ<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrype1840181_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrype1840181-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Cranmer's first contact with a Continental reformer was with <a href="/wiki/Simon_Grynaeus" title="Simon Grynaeus">Simon Grynaeus</a>, a humanist based in <a href="/wiki/Basel" title="Basel">Basel</a>, Switzerland, and a follower of the Swiss reformers, <a href="/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli" title="Huldrych Zwingli">Huldrych Zwingli</a> and <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Oecolampadius" title="Johannes Oecolampadius">Johannes Oecolampadius</a>. In mid-1531, Grynaeus took an extended visit to England to offer himself as an intermediary between the king and the Continental reformers. He struck up a friendship with Cranmer and after his return to Basel, he wrote about Cranmer to the German reformer <a href="/wiki/Martin_Bucer" title="Martin Bucer">Martin Bucer</a> in <a href="/wiki/Strasbourg" title="Strasbourg">Strasbourg</a>. Grynaeus' early contacts initiated Cranmer's eventual relationship with the Strasbourg and Swiss reformers.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1532, Cranmer was appointed the resident ambassador at the court of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V</a>. As the emperor travelled throughout his realm, Cranmer had to follow him to his residence in <a href="/wiki/Regensburg" title="Regensburg">Regensburg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He passed through the Lutheran city of <a href="/wiki/Nuremberg" title="Nuremberg">Nuremberg</a> and saw for the first time the effects of the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Reformation</a>. When the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Diet_(Holy_Roman_Empire)" title="Imperial Diet (Holy Roman Empire)">Imperial Diet</a> was moved to Nuremberg, he met the leading architect of the Nuremberg reforms, <a href="/wiki/Andreas_Osiander" title="Andreas Osiander">Andreas Osiander</a>. They became good friends, and during that July Cranmer took the surprising action of marrying <a href="/wiki/Margarete_Cranmer" title="Margarete Cranmer">Margarete</a>, the niece of Osiander's wife. He did not take her as his mistress, as was the prevailing custom with priests for whom celibacy was too rigorous. Scholars note that Cranmer had moved, however moderately at this stage, into identifying with certain Lutheran principles.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> This progress in his personal life was not matched in his political life as he was unable to persuade Charles, Catherine's nephew, to support the annulment of his aunt's marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Appointed_Archbishop_of_Canterbury_.281532.E2.80.931534.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointed_Archbishop_of_Canterbury_(1532–1534)">Appointed Archbishop of Canterbury (1532–1534)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Appointed Archbishop of Canterbury (1532–1534)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>While Cranmer was following Charles through Italy, he received a royal letter dated 1 October 1532 informing him that he had been appointed the new Archbishop of Canterbury, following the death of archbishop <a href="/wiki/William_Warham" title="William Warham">William Warham</a>. Cranmer was ordered to return to England. The appointment had been secured by the family of <a href="/wiki/Anne_Boleyn" title="Anne Boleyn">Anne Boleyn</a>, who was being courted by Henry. When Cranmer's promotion became known in London, it caused great surprise as Cranmer had previously held only minor positions in the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer left <a href="/wiki/Mantua" title="Mantua">Mantua</a> on 19 November and arrived in England at the beginning of January.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Henry personally financed the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bulls" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal bulls">papal bulls</a> necessary for Cranmer's promotion to Canterbury. The bulls were easily acquired because the <a href="/wiki/Papal_nuncio" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal nuncio">papal nuncio</a> was under orders from Rome to please the English in an effort to prevent a final breach. The bulls arrived around 26 March 1533 and Cranmer was consecrated as a bishop on 30 March in <a href="/wiki/St_Stephen%27s_Chapel" title="St Stephen&#39;s Chapel">St Stephen's Chapel</a>, by <a href="/wiki/John_Longland" title="John Longland">John Longland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Lincoln" title="Bishop of Lincoln">Bishop of Lincoln</a>; <a href="/wiki/John_Vesey" title="John Vesey">John Vesey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Exeter" title="Bishop of Exeter">Bishop of Exeter</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Standish" title="Henry Standish">Henry Standish</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_St_Asaph" title="Bishop of St Asaph">Bishop of St Asaph</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerceval1841&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsanapologyfordoc00socigoogpagen204_188&#93;_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerceval1841[httpsarchiveorgdetailsanapologyfordoc00socigoogpagen204_188]-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Even while they were waiting for the bulls, Cranmer continued to work on the annulment proceedings, which required greater urgency after Anne announced her pregnancy. Henry and Anne were secretly married on 24 or 25 January 1533 in the presence of a handful of witnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer did not learn of the marriage until 14 days later.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Anne_boleyn.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Anne_boleyn.jpg/170px-Anne_boleyn.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Anne_boleyn.jpg/255px-Anne_boleyn.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Anne_boleyn.jpg/340px-Anne_boleyn.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2401" data-file-height="3150" /></a><figcaption>The family of <a href="/wiki/Anne_Boleyn" title="Anne Boleyn">Anne Boleyn</a> secured the appointment of Cranmer as Archbishop of Canterbury. Later portrait by an unknown artist.</figcaption></figure>
<p>For the next few months, Cranmer and the king worked on establishing legal procedures on how the monarch's marriage would be judged by his most senior clergy. Several drafts of the procedures have been preserved in letters written between the two. Once procedures were agreed upon, Cranmer opened court sessions on 10 May, inviting Henry and Catherine of Aragon to appear. Gardiner represented the king; Catherine did not appear or send a proxy. On 23 May Cranmer pronounced the judgement that Henry's marriage with Catherine was against the law of God. He even issued a threat of <a href="/wiki/Excommunication" title="Excommunication">excommunication</a> if Henry did not stay away from Catherine.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Henry was now free to marry and, on 28 May, Cranmer validated Henry and Anne's marriage. On 1 June, Cranmer personally crowned and anointed Anne queen and delivered to her the <a href="/wiki/Crown_Jewels_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Crown Jewels of the United Kingdom">sceptre and rod</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Pope <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Clement VII</a> was furious at this defiance, but he could not take decisive action as he was pressured by other monarchs to avoid an irreparable breach with England. On 9 July he provisionally excommunicated Henry and his advisers (who included Cranmer) unless he repudiated Anne by the end of September. Henry kept Anne as his wife and, on 7 September, Anne gave birth to <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_I_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth I of England">Elizabeth</a>. Cranmer <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptised</a> her immediately afterwards and acted as one of her godparents.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is difficult to assess how Cranmer's theological views had evolved since his Cambridge days. There is evidence that he continued to support humanism; he renewed Erasmus' pension that had previously been granted by Archbishop Warham.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In June 1533, he was confronted with the difficult tasks not only of disciplining a reformer, but also of seeing him burned at the stake. <a href="/wiki/John_Frith_(martyr)" title="John Frith (martyr)">John Frith</a> was condemned to death for his views on the eucharist: he denied the <a href="/wiki/Real_presence" class="mw-redirect" title="Real presence">real presence</a>. Cranmer personally tried to persuade him to change his views without success.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Although he rejected Frith's radicalism, by 1534 he clearly signalled that he had broken with Rome and that he had set a new theological course. He supported the cause of reform by gradually replacing the old guard in his <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_province" title="Ecclesiastical province">ecclesiastical province</a> with men such as <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Latimer" title="Hugh Latimer">Hugh Latimer</a> who followed the new thinking.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> He intervened in religious disputes, supporting reformers, to the disappointment of religious conservatives who desired to maintain the link with Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Under_the_vicegerency_.281535.E2.80.931538.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Under_the_vicegerency_(1535–1538)">Under the vicegerency (1535–1538)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Under the vicegerency (1535–1538)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cromwell,Thomas(1EEssex)01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg/170px-Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="203" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg/255px-Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg/340px-Cromwell%2CThomas%281EEssex%2901.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2768" data-file-height="3312" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cromwell" title="Thomas Cromwell">Thomas Cromwell</a> was the vicegerent acting as the main agent for the king over spiritual matters. Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Hans_Holbein_the_Younger" title="Hans Holbein the Younger">Hans Holbein</a>, 1532–1533.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Cranmer was not immediately accepted by the bishops within his province. When he attempted a <a href="/wiki/Canonical_visitation" title="Canonical visitation">canonical visitation</a>, he had to avoid locations where a resident conservative bishop might make an embarrassing personal challenge to his authority. In 1535, Cranmer had difficult encounters with several bishops, <a href="/wiki/John_Stokesley" title="John Stokesley">John Stokesley</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Longland" title="John Longland">John Longland</a>, and Stephen Gardiner among others. They objected to Cranmer's power and title and argued that the <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Supremacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Act of Supremacy">Act of Supremacy</a> did not define his role. This prompted <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cromwell" title="Thomas Cromwell">Thomas Cromwell</a>, the king's chief minister, to activate and to take the office of the <a href="/wiki/Vicegerent" title="Vicegerent">vicegerent</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> the deputy supreme head of ecclesiastical affairs. He created another set of institutions that gave a clear structure to the royal supremacy. Hence, the archbishop was eclipsed by Vicegerent Cromwell in regard to the king's spiritual jurisdiction.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> There is no evidence that Cranmer resented his position as junior partner.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Although he was an exceptional scholar, he lacked the political ability to outface even clerical opponents. Those tasks were left to Cromwell.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 29 January 1536, when Anne miscarried a son, the king began to reflect again on the biblical prohibitions that had haunted him during his marriage with Catherine of Aragon.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after the miscarriage, the king started to take an interest in <a href="/wiki/Jane_Seymour" title="Jane Seymour">Jane Seymour</a>. By 24 April, he had commissioned Cromwell to prepare the case for a divorce.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Unaware of these plans, Cranmer had continued to write letters to Cromwell on minor matters up to 22 April. Anne was sent to the <a href="/wiki/Tower_of_London" title="Tower of London">Tower of London</a> on 2 May, and Cranmer was urgently summoned by Cromwell. On the very next day, Cranmer wrote a letter to the king expressing his doubts about the queen's guilt, highlighting his own esteem for Anne. After it was delivered, Cranmer was resigned to the fact that the end of Anne's marriage was inevitable.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> On 16 May, he saw Anne in the Tower and heard her confession and the following day, he pronounced the marriage null and void. Two days later, Anne was executed; Cranmer was one of the few who publicly mourned her death.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The vicegerency brought the pace of reforms under the control of the king. A balance was instituted between the conservatives and the reformers and this was seen in the <i><a href="/wiki/Ten_Articles" class="mw-redirect" title="Ten Articles">Ten Articles</a></i>, the first attempt at defining the beliefs of the Henrician Church. The articles had a two-part structure. The first five articles showed the influence of the reformers by recognising only three of the <a href="/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church" title="Sacraments of the Catholic Church">former seven</a> <a href="/wiki/Sacraments" class="mw-redirect" title="Sacraments">sacraments</a>: baptism, eucharist, and <a href="/wiki/Penance" title="Penance">penance</a>. The last five articles concerned the roles of <a href="/wiki/Religious_images" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious images">images</a>, <a href="/wiki/Veneration" title="Veneration">saints</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ritual" title="Ritual">rites</a> and ceremonies, and <a href="/wiki/Purgatory" title="Purgatory">purgatory</a>, and they reflected the views of the traditionalists. Two early drafts of the document have been preserved and show different teams of theologians at work. The competition between the conservatives and reformers is revealed in rival editorial corrections made by Cranmer and <a href="/wiki/Cuthbert_Tunstall" title="Cuthbert Tunstall">Cuthbert Tunstall</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Durham" title="Bishop of Durham">bishop of Durham</a>. The end product had something that pleased and annoyed both sides of the debate.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> By 11 July, Cranmer, Cromwell, and the <a href="/wiki/Convocation_of_the_English_Clergy" class="mw-redirect" title="Convocation of the English Clergy">Convocation</a>, the general assembly of the clergy, had subscribed to the <i>Ten Articles</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late 1536, the north of England was convulsed in a series of uprisings collectively known as the <a href="/wiki/Pilgrimage_of_Grace" title="Pilgrimage of Grace">Pilgrimage of Grace</a>, the most serious opposition to Henry's policies. Cromwell and Cranmer were the primary targets of the protesters' fury. Cromwell and the king worked furiously to quell the rebellion, while Cranmer kept a low profile.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> After it was clear that Henry's regime was safe, the government took the initiative to remedy the evident inadequacy of the <i>Ten Articles</i>. The outcome after months of debate was <i><a href="/wiki/The_Institution_of_a_Christian_Man" class="mw-redirect" title="The Institution of a Christian Man">The Institution of a Christian Man</a></i> informally known from the first issue as the Bishops' Book. The book was initially proposed in February 1537 in the first vicegerential synod, ordered by Cromwell, for the whole Church. Cromwell opened the proceedings, but as the synod progressed, Cranmer and Foxe took on the chairmanship and the co-ordination. Foxe did most of the final editing and the book was published in late September.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Even after publication, the book's status remained vague because the king had not given his full support to it. In a draft letter, Henry noted that he had not read the book, but supported its printing. His attention was most likely occupied by the pregnancy of Jane Seymour and the birth of the male heir, <a href="/wiki/Edward_VI_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VI of England">Edward</a>, that Henry had sought for so long. Jane died shortly after giving birth and her funeral was held on 12 November. That month Henry started to work on the Bishops' Book; his amendments were sent to Cranmer, Sampson, and others for comment. Cranmer's responses to the king were far more confrontational than his colleagues' and he wrote at much greater length.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> They reveal unambiguous statements supporting reformed theology such as justification by faith or <i><a href="/wiki/Sola_fide" title="Sola fide">sola fide</a></i> (faith alone) and <a href="/wiki/Predestination" title="Predestination">predestination</a>. His words did not convince the king. A new statement of faith was delayed until 1543 with the publication of the King's Book.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1538, the king and Cromwell arranged with Lutheran princes to have detailed discussions on forming a political and religious alliance. Henry had been seeking a new embassy from the <a href="/wiki/Schmalkaldic_League" title="Schmalkaldic League">Schmalkaldic League</a> since mid-1537. The Lutherans were delighted by this and they sent a joint delegation from various German cities, including a colleague of Martin Luther's, <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Myconius" title="Friedrich Myconius">Friedrich Myconius</a>. The delegates arrived in England on 27 May 1538. After initial meetings with the king, Cromwell, and Cranmer, discussions on theological differences were transferred to <a href="/wiki/Lambeth_Palace" title="Lambeth Palace">Lambeth Palace</a> under Cranmer's chairmanship. Progress on an agreement was slow partly owing to Cromwell being too busy to help expedite the proceedings and partly because the negotiating team on the English side was evenly balanced between conservatives and reformers. The talks dragged on with the Germans becoming weary despite the Archbishop's strenuous efforts. The negotiations were fatally neutralised by an appointee of the king. Cranmer's colleague, Edward Foxe, who sat on Henry's <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council">Privy Council</a>, had died earlier in the year. The king chose as his replacement Cranmer's conservative rival, Cuthbert Tunstall, who was told to stay near Henry to give advice. On 5 August, when the German delegates sent a letter to the king regarding three items that particularly worried them (compulsory clerical celibacy, the withholding of the chalice from the laity, and the maintenance of <a href="/wiki/Sine_populo" title="Sine populo">private masses</a> for the dead), Tunstall was able to intervene for the king and to influence the decision. The result was a thorough dismissal by the king of many of the Germans' chief concerns. Although Cranmer begged the Germans to continue with the negotiations, using the argument "to consider the many thousands of souls in England" at stake, they left on 1 October without any substantial achievements.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Reforms_reversed_.281539.E2.80.931542.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Reforms_reversed_(1539–1542)">Reforms reversed (1539–1542)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Reforms reversed (1539–1542)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Continental reformer <a href="/wiki/Philipp_Melanchthon" class="mw-redirect" title="Philipp Melanchthon">Philipp Melanchthon</a> was aware that he was very much admired by Henry. In early 1539, Melanchthon wrote several letters to Henry criticising his views on religion, in particular his support of clerical celibacy. By late April another delegation from the Lutheran princes arrived to build on Melanchthon's exhortations. Cromwell wrote a letter to the king in support of the new Lutheran mission. The king had begun to change his stance and concentrated on wooing conservative opinion in England rather than reaching out to the Lutherans. On 28 April 1539, Parliament met for the first time in three years. Cranmer was present, but Cromwell was unable to attend due to ill health. On 5 May the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> created a committee with the customary religious balance between conservatives and reformers to examine and determine doctrine. The committee was given little time to do the detailed work needed for a thorough revision. On 16 May, the <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Howard,_3rd_Duke_of_Norfolk" title="Thomas Howard, 3rd Duke of Norfolk">Duke of Norfolk</a> noted that the committee had not agreed on anything, and proposed that the Lords examine six doctrinal questions—which eventually formed the basis of the <i><a href="/wiki/Six_Articles_(1539)" class="mw-redirect" title="Six Articles (1539)">Six Articles</a></i>. They affirmed the conservative interpretation of doctrines such as the real presence, clerical celibacy, and the necessity of auricular confession, the private confession of sins to a priest.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> As the Act of the Six Articles neared passage in Parliament, Cranmer moved his wife and children out of England to safety. Up until this time, the family was kept quietly hidden, most likely in <a href="/wiki/Ford_Palace" title="Ford Palace">Ford Palace</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kent" title="Kent">Kent</a>. The Act passed Parliament at the end of June and it forced Latimer and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Shaxton" title="Nicholas Shaxton">Nicholas Shaxton</a> to resign their dioceses given their outspoken opposition to the measure.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg/170px-ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg/255px-ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg/340px-ADurerMelancthonengraving1526.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1092" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Philipp_Melanchthon" class="mw-redirect" title="Philipp Melanchthon">Philipp Melanchthon</a> was the Continental reformer Henry most admired.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> In 1552 Cranmer invited him to participate in an ecumenical council in England. Engraving by <a href="/wiki/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer" title="Albrecht Dürer">Albrecht Dürer</a>, 1526</figcaption></figure>
<p>The setback for the reformers was short-lived. By September, Henry was displeased with the results of the Act and its promulgators; the ever-loyal Cranmer and Cromwell were back in favour. The king asked his archbishop to write a new preface for the <a href="/wiki/Great_Bible" title="Great Bible">Great Bible</a>, an English translation of the Bible that was first published in April 1539 under the direction of Cromwell. The preface was in the form of a sermon addressed to readers. As for Cromwell, he was delighted that his plan of a royal marriage between Henry and <a href="/wiki/Anne_of_Cleves" title="Anne of Cleves">Anne of Cleves</a>, the sister of a German prince was accepted by the king. In Cromwell's view, the marriage could potentially bring back contacts with the <a href="/wiki/Schmalkaldic_League" title="Schmalkaldic League">Schmalkaldic League</a>. Henry was dismayed with Anne when they first met on 1 January 1540 but married her reluctantly on 6 January in a ceremony officiated by Cranmer. The marriage ended in disaster as Henry decided that he would request a royal divorce. This resulted in Henry being placed in an embarrassing position and Cromwell suffered the consequences. His old enemies, including the Duke of Norfolk, took advantage of the weakened Cromwell and he was arrested on 10 June. He immediately lost the support of all his friends, including Cranmer. As Cranmer had done for Anne Boleyn, he wrote a letter to the king defending the past work of Cromwell. Henry's marriage to Anne of Cleves was quickly annulled on 9 July by the vice-gerential synod, now led by Cranmer and Gardiner.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the annulment, Cromwell was executed on 28 July.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer now found himself in a politically prominent position, with no one else to shoulder the burden.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the rest of Henry's reign, he clung to Henry's authority. The king had total trust in him and in return, Cranmer could not conceal anything from the king.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> At the end of June 1541, Henry with his new wife, <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Howard" title="Catherine Howard">Catherine Howard</a>, left for his first visit to the north of England. Cranmer was left in London as a member of a council taking care of matters for the king in his absence. His colleagues were Lord Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Audley,_1st_Baron_Audley_of_Walden" title="Thomas Audley, 1st Baron Audley of Walden">Thomas Audley</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Seymour,_1st_Duke_of_Somerset" title="Edward Seymour, 1st Duke of Somerset">Edward Seymour</a>, Earl of Hertford. This was Cranmer's first major piece of responsibility outside the Church. In October, while the king and queen were away, a reformer named <a href="/wiki/John_Lascelles" class="mw-redirect" title="John Lascelles">John Lascelles</a> revealed to Cranmer that Catherine engaged in extramarital affairs. Cranmer gave the information to Audley and Seymour and they decided to wait until Henry's return. Afraid of angering the king, Audley and Seymour suggested that Cranmer inform Henry. Cranmer slipped a message to Henry during mass on <a href="/wiki/All_Saints_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="All Saints Day">All Saints Day</a>. An investigation revealed the truth of the marital indiscretions and Catherine was executed in February 1542.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Support_from_the_King_.281543.E2.80.931547.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_from_the_King_(1543–1547)">Support from the King (1543–1547)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Support from the King (1543–1547)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1543, several conservative clergymen in Kent banded together to attack and denounce two reformers, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Turner_(reformer)" title="Richard Turner (reformer)">Richard Turner</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Bland_(reformer)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="John Bland (reformer) (page does not exist)">John Bland</a>, before the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council">Privy Council</a>. They prepared articles to present to the council, but at the last moment, additional denunciations were added by Stephen Gardiner's nephew, <a href="/wiki/Germain_Gardiner" class="mw-redirect" title="Germain Gardiner">Germain Gardiner</a>. These new articles attacked Cranmer and listed his misdeeds back to 1541. This document and the actions that followed were the basis of the so-called <a href="/wiki/Prebendaries%27_Plot" title="Prebendaries&#39; Plot">Prebendaries' Plot</a>. The articles were delivered to the Council in London and were probably read on 22 April 1543. The king most likely saw the articles against Cranmer that night. The archbishop appeared unaware that an attack on his person was made. His commissioners in Lambeth dealt specifically with Turner's case where he was acquitted, much to the fury of the conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While the plot against Cranmer was proceeding, the reformers were being attacked on other fronts. On 20 April, the Convocation reconvened to consider the revision of the Bishops' Book. Cranmer presided over the sub-committees, but the conservatives were able to overturn many reforming ideas, including justification by faith <i>alone</i>. On 5 May, the new revision called <i><a href="/wiki/A_Necessary_Doctrine_and_Erudition_for_any_Christian_Man" class="mw-redirect" title="A Necessary Doctrine and Erudition for any Christian Man">A Necessary Doctrine and Erudition for any Christian Man</a></i> or the King's Book was released. Doctrinally, it was far more conservative than the Bishops' Book. On 10 May, the reformers received another blow. Parliament passed the Act for the Advancement of True Religion, which abolished "erroneous books" and restricted the reading of the Bible in English to those of noble status. From May to August, reformers were examined, forced to recant, or imprisoned.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For five months, Henry took no action on the accusations against his archbishop.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The conspiracy was finally revealed to Cranmer by the king himself. According to Cranmer's secretary, <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Morice" title="Ralph Morice">Ralph Morice</a>, sometime in September 1543 the king showed Cranmer a paper summarising the accusations against him. An investigation was to be mounted and Cranmer was appointed chief investigator. Surprise raids were carried out, evidence gathered, and ringleaders identified. Typically, Cranmer put the clergymen involved in the conspiracy through immediate humiliation, but he eventually forgave them and continued to use their services. To show his trust in Cranmer, Henry gave Cranmer his personal ring. When the Privy Council arrested Cranmer at the end of November, the nobles were stymied by the symbol of the king's trust in him.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer's victory ended with two second-rank leaders imprisoned and Germain Gardiner executed.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Cranmer.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Thomas_Cranmer.png/170px-Thomas_Cranmer.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Thomas_Cranmer.png/255px-Thomas_Cranmer.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Thomas_Cranmer.png/340px-Thomas_Cranmer.png 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="983" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Cranmer painted by an unknown artist after <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII</a>'s death.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> It was said that his beard signified his mourning of the king and his rejection of the old Church.</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the atmosphere in Cranmer's favour, he pursued quiet efforts to reform the Church, particularly the liturgy. On 27 May 1544 the first officially authorised vernacular service was published, the processional service of intercession known as the <i><a href="/wiki/Exhortation_and_Litany" title="Exhortation and Litany">Exhortation and Litany</a></i>. It survives today with minor modifications in the <i>Book of Common Prayer</i>. The traditional <a href="/wiki/Litany" title="Litany">litany</a> uses <a href="/wiki/Invocation" title="Invocation">invocations</a> to saints, but Cranmer thoroughly reformed this aspect by providing no opportunity in the text for such <a href="/wiki/Veneration" title="Veneration">veneration</a>. Additional reformers were elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_England" title="House of Commons of England">House of Commons</a> and new legislation was introduced to curb the effects of the Act of the Six Articles and the Act for the Advancement of True Religion.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1546, the conservatives in a coalition including Gardiner, the Duke of Norfolk, the Lord Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Wriothesley,_1st_Earl_of_Southampton" title="Thomas Wriothesley, 1st Earl of Southampton">Wriothesley</a>, and the bishop of London, <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Bonner" title="Edmund Bonner">Edmund Bonner</a>, made one last attempt to challenge the reformers. Several reformers with links to Cranmer were targeted. Some such as Lascelles were burned at the stake. Powerful reform-minded nobles Edward Seymour and <a href="/wiki/John_Dudley,_1st_Duke_of_Northumberland" title="John Dudley, 1st Duke of Northumberland">John Dudley</a> returned to England from overseas and they were able to turn the tide against the conservatives. Two incidents tipped the balance. Gardiner was disgraced before the king when he refused to agree to exchange episcopal estates, and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Howard,_Earl_of_Surrey" title="Henry Howard, Earl of Surrey">the son of the Duke of Norfolk</a> was charged with treason and executed. There is no evidence that Cranmer played any part in these political games, and there were no further plots as the king's health ebbed in his final months. Cranmer performed his final duties for the king on 28 January 1547 when he gave a reformed statement of faith while gripping Henry's hand instead of giving him his <a href="/wiki/Last_rites" title="Last rites">last rites</a>. Cranmer mourned Henry's death and it was later said that he demonstrated his grief by growing a beard. The beard was also a sign of his break with the past. Continental reformers grew beards to mark their rejection of the old Church and this significance of clerical beards was well understood in England. On 31 January, he was among the <a href="/wiki/Executor" title="Executor">executors</a> of the king's final will that nominated Edward Seymour as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Protector" title="Lord Protector">Lord Protector</a> and welcomed the boy king, <a href="/wiki/Edward_VI_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VI of England">Edward VI</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Foreign_divines_and_reformed_doctrines_.281547.E2.80.931549.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_divines_and_reformed_doctrines_(1547–1549)">Foreign divines and reformed doctrines (1547–1549)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Foreign divines and reformed doctrines (1547–1549)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin-Bucer_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Martin-Bucer_1.jpg/170px-Martin-Bucer_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Martin-Bucer_1.jpg/255px-Martin-Bucer_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Martin-Bucer_1.jpg/340px-Martin-Bucer_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1450" data-file-height="1902" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Martin_Bucer" title="Martin Bucer">Martin Bucer</a>, who had corresponded with Cranmer for many years, was forced to take refuge in England.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Under the regency of Seymour, the reformers became part of the establishment. A royal visitation of the provinces took place in August 1547 and each parish that was visited was instructed to obtain a copy of the <i>Homilies</i>. This book consisted of twelve homilies of which four were written by Cranmer. His reassertion of the doctrine of justification by faith elicited a strong reaction from Gardiner.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> In the "Homily of Good Works annexed to Faith", Cranmer attacked <a href="/wiki/Christian_monasticism" title="Christian monasticism">monasticism</a> and the importance of various personal actions involved in liturgical recitations and ceremonies. Hence, he narrowed the range of good works that would be considered necessary and reinforced the primacy of faith. In each parish visited, injunctions were put in place that resolved to, "...eliminate any image which had any suspicion of devotion attached to it."<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cranmer's eucharistic views, which had already moved away from official Catholic doctrine, received another push from Continental reformers. Cranmer had been in contact with Martin Bucer since the time when initial contacts were made with the Schmalkaldic League. Cranmer and Bucer's relationship became ever closer owing to Charles V's victory over the League at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_M%C3%BChlberg" title="Battle of Mühlberg">Mühlberg</a>, which left England as the sole major nation that gave sanctuary to persecuted reformers. Cranmer wrote a letter to Bucer (now lost) with questions on eucharistic theology. In Bucer's reply dated 28 November 1547, he denied the corporeal real presence and condemned <a href="/wiki/Transubstantiation" title="Transubstantiation">transubstantiation</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Adoration" title="Adoration">adoration</a> of the elements. The letter was delivered to Cranmer by two Italian reformed theologians, <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Martire_Vermigli" class="mw-redirect" title="Pietro Martire Vermigli">Peter Martyr</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bernardino_Ochino" title="Bernardino Ochino">Bernardino Ochino</a> who were invited to take refuge in England. Martyr also brought with him an epistle written allegedly by <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a>, <i>Ad Caesarium Monachum</i>, which appeared to provide <a href="/wiki/Patristic" class="mw-redirect" title="Patristic">patristic</a> support against the corporeal real presence.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> These documents influenced Cranmer's thoughts on the eucharist.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1549, the city of Strasbourg forced Martin Bucer and <a href="/wiki/Paul_Fagius" title="Paul Fagius">Paul Fagius</a> to leave. Cranmer immediately invited the men to come to England and promised that they would be placed in English universities. When they arrived on 25 April, Cranmer was especially delighted to meet Bucer face to face after eighteen years of correspondence.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> He needed these scholarly men to train a new generation of preachers as well as to assist in the reform of liturgy and doctrine. Others who accepted his invitations included the Polish reformer, <a href="/wiki/Jan_%C5%81aski" title="Jan Łaski">Jan Łaski</a>, but Cranmer was unable to convince Osiander and Melanchthon that they should come to England.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Book_of_Common_Prayer_.281548.E2.80.931549.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Book_of_Common_Prayer_(1548–1549)"><i>Book of Common Prayer</i> (1548–1549)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Book of Common Prayer (1548–1549)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Book_of_Common_Prayer,_1549_(2).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg/220px-Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="340" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg/330px-Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg/440px-Book_of_Common_Prayer%2C_1549_%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1296" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>The title page of the 1549 <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer" title="Book of Common Prayer">Book of Common Prayer</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>As the use of English in worship services spread, the need for a complete uniform liturgy for the Church became evident. Initial meetings to start what would eventually become the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer_(1549)" title="Book of Common Prayer (1549)">1549 <i>Book of Common Prayer</i></a> were held in the former <a href="/wiki/Chertsey_Abbey" title="Chertsey Abbey">abbey of Chertsey</a> and in <a href="/wiki/Windsor_Castle" title="Windsor Castle">Windsor Castle</a> in September 1548. The list of participants can only be partially reconstructed, but it is known that the members were balanced between conservatives and reformers. These meetings were followed by a debate on the Eucharist in the House of Lords which took place between 14 and 19 December. Cranmer publicly revealed in this debate that he had abandoned the doctrine of the corporeal real presence and believed that the Eucharistic presence was only spiritual.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Parliament backed the publication of the Prayer Book after Christmas by passing the <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Uniformity_1549" class="mw-redirect" title="Act of Uniformity 1549">Act of Uniformity 1549</a>; it then legalized clerical marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is difficult to ascertain how much of the Prayer Book is Cranmer's personal composition. Generations of liturgical scholars have been able to track down the sources that he used, including the <a href="/wiki/Sarum_Rite" class="mw-redirect" title="Sarum Rite">Sarum Rite</a>, writings from <a href="/wiki/Hermann_of_Wied" title="Hermann of Wied">Hermann von Wied</a>, and several Lutheran sources including Osiander and <a href="/wiki/Justus_Jonas" title="Justus Jonas">Justus Jonas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> It is more problematic to determine how Cranmer worked on the book and with whom he worked. Where information about his possible helpers is lacking, he is given the credit for the editorship and the overall structure of the book.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The use of the new Prayer Book was made compulsory on 9 June 1549. This triggered a series of protests in <a href="/wiki/Devon" title="Devon">Devon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cornwall" title="Cornwall">Cornwall</a> where the English language was not yet in common usage,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMills2010189Genocide_and_Ethnocide_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMills2010189Genocide_and_Ethnocide-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> now known as the <a href="/wiki/Prayer_Book_Rebellion" title="Prayer Book Rebellion">Prayer Book Rebellion</a>. By early July, the uprising had spread to other parts in the east of England. The rebels made a number of demands including the restoration of the Six Articles, the use of Latin for the mass with only the consecrated bread given to the laity, the restoration of prayers for souls in purgatory, and the rebuilding of abbeys. Cranmer wrote a strong response to these demands to the King in which he denounced the wickedness of the rebellion.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> On 21 July, Cranmer commandeered <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a> where he vigorously defended the official Church line. A draft of his sermon, the only extant written sample of his preaching from his entire career, shows that he collaborated with Peter Martyr on dealing with the rebellion.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Consolidating_gains_.281549.E2.80.931551.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Consolidating_gains_(1549–1551)">Consolidating gains (1549–1551)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Consolidating gains (1549–1551)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Prayer Book Rebellion and other events had a negative effect on the <a href="/wiki/Edward_Seymour,_1st_Duke_of_Somerset#Council_of_Regency" title="Edward Seymour, 1st Duke of Somerset">Seymour regency</a>. The Privy Council became divided when a set of dissident Councillors banded together behind John Dudley in order to oust Seymour. Cranmer and two other Councillors, <a href="/wiki/William_Paget,_1st_Baron_Paget" title="William Paget, 1st Baron Paget">William Paget</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Smith_(diplomat)" title="Thomas Smith (diplomat)">Thomas Smith</a> initially rallied behind Seymour. After a flurry of letters passed between the two sides, a bloodless <i>coup d'état</i> resulted in the end of Seymour's Protectorship on 13 October 1549. Despite the support of religiously conservative politicians behind Dudley's coup, the reformers managed to maintain control of the new government and the English Reformation continued to consolidate gains.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Seymour was initially imprisoned in the Tower, but he was shortly released on 6 February 1550 and returned to the council. The archbishop was able to transfer his former chaplain, <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Ridley_(martyr)" title="Nicholas Ridley (martyr)">Nicholas Ridley</a> from the minor <a href="/wiki/See_of_Rochester" class="mw-redirect" title="See of Rochester">see of Rochester</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_London" title="Diocese of London">diocese of London</a>, while <a href="/wiki/John_Ponet" title="John Ponet">John Ponet</a> took Ridley's former position. Incumbent conservatives were uprooted and replaced with reformers.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Hooper_by_Henry_Bryan_Hall_after_James_Warren_Childe_cropped.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/John_Hooper_by_Henry_Bryan_Hall_after_James_Warren_Childe_cropped.jpg/170px-John_Hooper_by_Henry_Bryan_Hall_after_James_Warren_Childe_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fa/John_Hooper_by_Henry_Bryan_Hall_after_James_Warren_Childe_cropped.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="232" data-file-height="304" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/John_Hooper_(bishop)" title="John Hooper (bishop)">John Hooper</a> was influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli" title="Huldrych Zwingli">Zwinglian</a> Reformation and advocated more radical reforms. Portrait by Henry Bryan Hall, 1839.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The first result of co-operation and consultation between Cranmer and Bucer was the Ordinal, the liturgy for the ordination of priests. This was missing in the first Prayer Book and was not published until 1550. Cranmer adopted Bucer's draft and created three services for commissioning a deacon, a priest, and a bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAvis200597–99_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAvis200597–99-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In the same year, Cranmer produced the <i><a href="/wiki/Defence_of_the_True_and_Catholic_Doctrine_of_the_Sacrament_of_the_Body_and_Blood_of_Christ" title="Defence of the True and Catholic Doctrine of the Sacrament of the Body and Blood of Christ">Defence of the True and Catholic Doctrine of the Sacrament of the Body and Blood of Christ</a></i>, a semi-official explanation of the eucharistic theology within the Prayer Book. It was the first full-length book to bear Cranmer's name on the title-page. The preface summarises his quarrel with Rome in a well-known passage where he compared "beads, pardons, pilgrimages, and such other like popery" with weeds, but the roots of the weeds were transubstantiation, the corporeal real presence, and the sacrificial nature of the mass.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Bucer assisted in the development of the English Reformation, he was still quite concerned about the speed of its progress. Both Bucer and Fagius had noticed that the 1549 Prayer Book was not a remarkable step forward, although Cranmer assured Bucer that it was only a first step and that its initial form was only temporary.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> By late 1550, Bucer was becoming disillusioned. Cranmer made sure that he did not feel alienated and kept in close touch with him. This attention paid off during the <a href="/wiki/Vestments_controversy" title="Vestments controversy">vestments controversy</a>. This incident was initiated by <a href="/wiki/John_Hooper_(bishop)" title="John Hooper (bishop)">John Hooper</a>, a follower of <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Bullinger" title="Heinrich Bullinger">Heinrich Bullinger</a> who had recently returned from <a href="/wiki/Z%C3%BCrich" title="Zürich">Zürich</a>. Hooper was unhappy with Cranmer's Prayer Book and Ordinal and he particularly objected to the use of ceremonies and vestments. When the Privy Council selected him to be the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Gloucester" title="Bishop of Gloucester">Bishop of Gloucester</a> on 15 May 1550, he laid down conditions that he would not wear the required vestments. He found an ally among the Continental reformers in Jan Łaski who had become a leader of the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Church,_Austin_Friars" title="Dutch Church, Austin Friars">Stranger church</a> in London, a designated place of worship for Continental Protestant refugees. His church's forms and practices had taken reforms much further than Cranmer would have liked. Bucer and Peter Martyr, while they sympathised with Hooper's position, supported Cranmer's arguments of timing and authority. Cranmer and Ridley stood their ground. This led to Hooper's imprisonment and he eventually gave in. He was consecrated on 8 March 1551 according to the Ordinal and he preached before the king in his episcopal garments. Cranmer's vision of reform through careful steps under the authority of the government was maintained.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Final_reform_programme_.281551.E2.80.931553.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_reform_programme_(1551–1553)">Final reform programme (1551–1553)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Final reform programme (1551–1553)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer_(1552)" title="Book of Common Prayer (1552)">Book of Common Prayer (1552)</a></div>
<p>Cranmer's role in politics was diminishing when on 16 October 1551 Seymour was arrested on charges of treason. In December Seymour was put on trial and although acquitted of treason, he was judged guilty of <a href="/wiki/Felony" title="Felony">felony</a> and was put to death on 22 January 1552.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> This was the beginning of the breach between Cranmer and Dudley. It was aggravated during the year by the gradual appropriation of ecclesiastical property by the regency.<sup id="cite_ref-MacCulloch520_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacCulloch520-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Throughout this political turmoil, Cranmer worked simultaneously on three major projects in his reform programme: the revision of <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a>, the revision of the Prayer Book, and the formation of a statement of doctrine.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
