<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg/170px-Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg/255px-Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg/340px-Pietro_Vermigli_by_Hans_Asper.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2923" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Peter_Martyr_Vermigli" title="Peter Martyr Vermigli">Peter Martyr (Pietro Martire Vermigli)</a>  greatly assisted Cranmer in the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">English Reformation</a>. Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Hans_Asper" title="Hans Asper">Hans Asper</a>, 1560.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The original Catholic canon law that defined governance within the Church clearly needed revision following Henry's break with Rome. Several revision attempts were made throughout Henry's reign, but these initial projects were shelved as the speed of reform outpaced the time required to work on a revision. As the reformation stabilised, Cranmer formed a committee in December 1551 to restart the work. He recruited Peter Martyr to the committee and he also asked Łaski and Hooper to participate, showing his habitual ability to forgive past actions. Cranmer and Martyr realised that a successful enactment of a reformed ecclesiastical law-code in England would have international significance. Cranmer planned to draw together all the reformed churches of Europe under England's leadership to counter the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a>, the Catholic Church's response to the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a>. In March 1552, Cranmer invited the foremost Continental reformers, Bullinger, <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a>, and Melanchthon to come to England and to participate in an ecumenical council.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The response was disappointing: Melanchthon did not respond, Bullinger stated that neither of them could leave Germany as it was riven by war between the Emperor and the Lutheran princes, and while Calvin showed some enthusiasm, he said he was unable to come. Cranmer acknowledged Calvin and replied stating, "Meanwhile, we will reform the English Church to the utmost of our ability and give our labour that both its doctrines and laws will be improved after the model of holy scripture."<sup id="cite_ref-MacCulloch520_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacCulloch520-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> One partial manuscript of the project survived that was annotated with corrections and comments by Cranmer and Martyr. When the final version was presented to Parliament, the breach between Cranmer and Dudley was complete and the regent effectively killed the canon law bill in the House of Lords.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As with the first Prayer Book, the origins and participants in the work of its revision are obscure, but it was clear that Cranmer led the project and steered its development. It had begun as early as the end of 1549 when the Convocation of Canterbury met to discuss the matter. Late in 1550, the opinions of Martyr and Bucer were sought on how the liturgy might be improved and they influenced the revision significantly.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> The view of the spiritual presence was clarified by the use of entirely different words at the offering of the bread and the wine to the communicants. New <a href="/wiki/Rubrics" class="mw-redirect" title="Rubrics">rubrics</a> noted that any kind of bread could be used and any bread or wine that remained could be used by the <a href="/wiki/Curate" title="Curate">curate</a>, thus dissociating the elements from any physical presence. The new book removed any possibility of prayers for the dead, because such prayers implied support for the doctrine of purgatory.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Uniformity_1552" title="Act of Uniformity 1552">Act of Uniformity 1552</a>, which authorised the book's use, specified that it be exclusively used from 1 November. The final version was officially published at almost the last minute, owing to Dudley's intervention. While travelling in the north of the country he met the Scots reformer <a href="/wiki/John_Knox" title="John Knox">John Knox</a>, then based in <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne" title="Newcastle upon Tyne">Newcastle</a>. Impressed by his preaching, Dudley selected him to be a royal chaplain and brought him south to participate in the reform projects. In a sermon before the king, Knox attacked the practice of kneeling during communion. On 27 September 1552, the Privy Council stopped the printing of the new Prayer Book and told Cranmer to revise it. He responded with a long letter using the argument that it was for Parliament with the royal assent to decide any changes in the liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> On 22 October, the council decided to keep the liturgy as it was and to add the so-called <i><a href="/wiki/Black_Rubric" title="Black Rubric">Black Rubric</a></i>, which explained that no adoration was intended when kneeling at communion.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The origins of the statement which eventually became the <i><a href="/wiki/Forty-two_Articles" title="Forty-two Articles">Forty-two Articles</a></i> are equally obscure. As early as December 1549, the archbishop was demanding that his bishops should subscribe to certain doctrinal articles. In 1551 Cranmer presented a version of a statement to the bishops, but its status remained ambiguous. Cranmer did not devote much effort to developing the articles, most likely owing to work on the canon law revision. He became more interested once the hope for an ecumenical council began to fade. By September 1552, draft versions of the articles were being worked on by Cranmer and <a href="/wiki/John_Cheke" title="John Cheke">John Cheke</a>, his scholarly friend who was commissioned to translate them into Latin. When the <i>Forty-two Articles</i> were finally published in May 1553, the title-page declared that the articles were agreed upon by the Convocation and were published by the authority of the king. This was not in fact the case and the mistake was likely caused by miscommunications between the archbishop and the Privy Council. Cranmer complained about this to the council, but the authorities responded by noting that the articles were developed during the time of the Convocation (hence evading giving a direct answer). The council gave Cranmer the unhappy task of requiring the bishops to subscribe to the articles, many of whom opposed them and pointed out the anomaly of the title-page. It was while Cranmer was carrying out this duty that events unfolded which were to render the subscriptions futile.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Trials.2C_recantations.2C_execution_.281553.E2.80.931556.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Trials,_recantations,_execution_(1553–1556)">Trials, recantations, execution (1553–1556)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Trials, recantations, execution (1553–1556)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Christ_Church_(Episcopal),_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg/170px-Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="419" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg/255px-Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg/340px-Christ_Church_%28Episcopal%29%2C_Little_Rock_-_window.jpg 2x" data-file-width="346" data-file-height="852" /></a><figcaption>Stained glass window depicting Cranmer, <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Ridley_(martyr)" title="Nicholas Ridley (martyr)">Ridley</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Latimer" title="Hugh Latimer">Latimer</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_Martyrs" title="Oxford Martyrs">Oxford Martyrs</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Edward_VI_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VI of England">Edward VI</a> became seriously ill and the councillors were told that he did not have long to live. In May 1553, the council sent several letters to Continental reformers assuring them that Edward's health was improving. Among the letters was one addressed to Melanchthon inviting him to come to England to take up the <a href="/wiki/Regius_Professor_of_Divinity" title="Regius Professor of Divinity">Regius Chair</a> in Cambridge which was vacant since the death of Martin Bucer in February 1551. Both Henry VIII and Cranmer had previously failed to convince Melanchthon to come; this time, the council made a serious effort by sending him an advance to cover his travel expenses. Cranmer sent a personal letter urging him to take the offer. Despite his plea, Melanchthon never made the voyage to England. While this effort to shore up the reformation was taking place, the council was working to persuade several judges to put on the throne <a href="/wiki/Lady_Jane_Grey" title="Lady Jane Grey">Lady Jane Grey</a>, Edward's cousin and a Protestant, instead of Mary, Henry and Catherine of Aragon's daughter and a Catholic. On 17 June 1553, the king made his will, noting Jane would succeed him, contravening the <a href="/wiki/Third_Succession_Act" title="Third Succession Act">Third Succession Act</a>. Cranmer tried to speak to Edward alone, but he was refused and his audience with Edward occurred in the presence of the councillors. Edward told him that he supported what he had written in his will. Cranmer's decision to support Jane must have occurred before 19 June, when royal orders were sent to convene the Convocation for the recognition of the new succession.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By mid-July, there were serious provincial revolts in Mary's favour and support for Jane in the council fell. As Mary was proclaimed queen, Dudley, Ridley, Cheke, and Jane's father, the <a href="/wiki/Henry_Grey,_1st_Duke_of_Suffolk" title="Henry Grey, 1st Duke of Suffolk">Duke of Suffolk</a>, were imprisoned. No action was taken against the archbishop. On 8 August, he led Edward's funeral according to the rites of the Prayer Book. During these months, he advised others, including <a href="/wiki/Peter_Martyr_Vermigli" title="Peter Martyr Vermigli">Peter Martyr</a>, to flee England, but he himself chose to stay. Reformed bishops were removed from office and conservative clergy, such as Edmund Bonner, had their old positions restored. Cranmer did not go down without a fight. When rumours spread that he authorised the use of the mass in Canterbury Cathedral, he declared them to be false and said, "All the doctrine and religion by our said sovereign lord king Edward VI is more pure and according to God's word than any that hath been used in England these thousand years."<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Not surprisingly, the government regarded Cranmer's declaration as tantamount to sedition. He was ordered to stand before the council in the <a href="/wiki/Star_Chamber" title="Star Chamber">Star Chamber</a> on 14 September and on that day he said his final goodbye to Martyr. Cranmer was sent straight to the Tower to join Hugh Latimer and Nicholas Ridley.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 13 November 1553, Cranmer and four others were brought to trial for treason, found guilty, and condemned to death. Numerous witnesses testified that Cranmer had encouraged heresy and had written heretical works.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarshall2017397_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall2017397-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Through February 1554, Jane Grey and other rebels were executed, and attention then turned to the religious leaders of the reformation. On 8 March 1554, the Privy Council ordered Cranmer, Ridley, and Latimer to be transferred to <a href="/wiki/Bocardo_Prison" title="Bocardo Prison">Bocardo prison</a> in Oxford to await a second trial for heresy. During this time, Cranmer was able to smuggle out a letter to Martyr, who had fled to Strasbourg, the last surviving document written in his own hand. He stated that the desperate situation of the church was proof that it would eventually be delivered and wrote, "I pray that God may grant that we may endure to the end!"<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer remained isolated in Bocardo prison for seventeen months before the trial started on 12 September 1555. Although it took place in England, the trial was under papal jurisdiction and the final verdict would come from Rome. Under interrogation, Cranmer admitted to every fact that was placed before him, but he denied any treachery, disobedience, or heresy. The trial of Latimer and Ridley started shortly after Cranmer's but their verdicts came almost immediately and they were burned at the stake on 16 October. Cranmer was taken to a tower to watch the proceedings. On 4 December, Rome decided Cranmer's fate by <a href="/wiki/Benefit_of_clergy" title="Benefit of clergy">depriving him of the archbishopric</a> and giving permission to the secular authorities to carry out their sentence.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg/250px-Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="182" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg/375px-Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg/500px-Trial_of_Thomas_Cranmer.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1457" /></a><figcaption>The Trial of Thomas Cranmer (1580)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In his final days, Cranmer's circumstances changed, which led to several <a href="/wiki/Recantation" title="Recantation">recantations</a>. On 11 December, he was taken out of Bocardo and placed in the house of the Dean of <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church</a>. This new environment was very different from that of his two years in prison. He was in an academic community and treated as a guest. Approached by a Dominican friar, <a href="/wiki/Juan_de_Villagarc%C3%ADa" title="Juan de Villagarcía">Juan de Villagarcía</a>, he debated the issues of papal supremacy and purgatory. In his first four recantations, produced between the end of January and mid-February, Cranmer submitted himself to the authority of the king and queen and recognised the Pope as head of the Church. On 14 February 1556, he was degraded from holy orders and returned to Bocardo. He had conceded very little and Edmund Bonner was not satisfied with these admissions. 
</p><p>On 24 February, a writ was issued to the mayor of Oxford and the date of Cranmer's execution was set for 7 March. Two days after the writ was issued, a fifth statement, the first which could be called a true recantation, was issued. Cranmer repudiated all <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheran</a> and <a href="/wiki/Reformation_in_Switzerland" title="Reformation in Switzerland">Zwinglian theology</a>, fully accepted Catholic theology, including papal supremacy and transubstantiation, and stated that there was no salvation outside the Catholic Church. He announced his joy at returning to the Catholic faith, asked for and received sacramental <a href="/wiki/Absolution" title="Absolution">absolution</a>, and participated in the mass. Cranmer's burning was postponed and, under the normal practice of canon law, he should have been absolved. Mary, however, decided that no further postponement was possible. His last recantation was issued on 18 March. It was a sign of a broken man, a sweeping confession of sin.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Despite the stipulation in canon law that recanting heretics be reprieved, Mary was determined to make an example of Cranmer, arguing that "his iniquity and obstinacy was so great against God and your Grace that your clemency and mercy could have no place with him", and pressed ahead with his execution.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg/250px-Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="182" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg/375px-Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg/500px-Cranmer_burning_foxe.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1440" data-file-height="1048" /></a><figcaption>Cranmer's martyrdom, from <a href="/wiki/Foxe%27s_Book_of_Martyrs" title="Foxe&#39;s Book of Martyrs">Foxe's Book of Martyrs</a> (1563)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Cranmer was told that he would be able to make a final recantation, but that this time it was to be in public during a service at the <a href="/wiki/University_Church_of_St_Mary_the_Virgin" title="University Church of St Mary the Virgin">University Church</a>. He wrote and submitted the speech in advance and it was published after his death. At the pulpit on the day of his execution, 21 March 1556, he opened with a prayer and an exhortation to obey the king and queen, but he ended his sermon totally unexpectedly, deviating from the prepared script. He renounced the recantations that he had written or signed with his own hand since his degradation and he stated that, in consequence, his hand would be punished by being burnt first. He then said, "And as for the Pope, I refuse him, as Christ's enemy, and <a href="/wiki/Antichrist" title="Antichrist">Antichrist</a> with all his false doctrine."<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> He was pulled from the pulpit and taken to where Latimer and Ridley had been burnt six months previously. As the flames drew around him, he fulfilled his promise by placing his right hand into the heart of the fire, calling it "that unworthy hand". His <a href="/wiki/Last_words" title="Last words">dying words</a> were, "Lord Jesus, receive my spirit. I see the heavens open and Jesus standing at the right hand of God."<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath_and_legacy">Aftermath and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Aftermath and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Marian government produced a pamphlet with all six recantations plus the text of the speech Cranmer was to have made in the University Church. His subsequent withdrawal of his recantations was not mentioned, though what actually happened soon became common knowledge, undermining the effectiveness of Marian propaganda. Similarly, the Protestant party had difficulty in making use of the event, given Cranmer's recantations. The <a href="/wiki/Marian_exiles" title="Marian exiles">exiles</a>' propaganda concentrated on publishing various specimens of his writings. Eventually <a href="/wiki/John_Foxe" title="John Foxe">John Foxe</a> put Cranmer's story to effective use in 1559, and it featured prominently in his <i><a href="/wiki/Acts_and_Monuments" class="mw-redirect" title="Acts and Monuments">Acts and Monuments</a></i> when it was first printed in 1563.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial,_Oxford.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg/200px-Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg/300px-Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg/400px-Statue_of_Cranmer_on_the_Martyr%27s_Memorial%2C_Oxford.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="4896" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Cranmer on the <a href="/wiki/Martyrs%27_Memorial,_Oxford" title="Martyrs&#39; Memorial, Oxford">Martyrs' Memorial, Oxford</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Cranmer's family had been exiled to the Continent in 1539. It is not known exactly when they returned to England, but it was soon after the accession of Edward VI in 1547 that Cranmer publicly acknowledged their existence. Not much is known about the early years of the children. His daughter, Margaret, was likely born in the 1530s and his son, Thomas, came later, probably during the reign of Edward. Around the time of Mary's accession, Cranmer's wife, Margarete, escaped to Germany, while his son was entrusted to his brother, Edmund Cranmer, who took him to the Continent. Margarete Cranmer eventually married Cranmer's favourite publisher, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Whitchurch" title="Edward Whitchurch">Edward Whitchurch</a>. The couple returned to England after Mary's reign and settled in <a href="/wiki/Surrey" title="Surrey">Surrey</a>. Whitchurch also negotiated for the marriage of Margaret to <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Norton" title="Thomas Norton">Thomas Norton</a>. Whitchurch died in 1562 and Margarete married for the third time to Bartholomew Scott. She died in the 1570s. Both of Cranmer's children died without issue and his line became extinct.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_I_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth I of England">Elizabeth I</a> came to power in 1558 she restored the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>'s independence from Rome under the <a href="/wiki/Elizabethan_Religious_Settlement" title="Elizabethan Religious Settlement">Elizabethan Religious Settlement</a>. The church that she re-established represented, in effect, a snapshot of the Edwardian Church from September 1552. Thus the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer_(1559)" title="Book of Common Prayer (1559)">Elizabethan Prayer Book</a> was basically Cranmer's 1552 edition but without the "Black Rubric". In the <a href="/wiki/Convocation_of_1563" title="Convocation of 1563">Convocation of 1563</a> the <i>Forty-two Articles</i> which were never adopted by the Church were altered in the area of eucharistic doctrine to form the <i>Thirty-Nine Articles</i>. Most of the exiles returned to England and resumed their careers in the Church. To some like <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Grindal" title="Edmund Grindal">Edmund Grindal</a>, an Archbishop of Canterbury during Elizabeth's reign, Cranmer provided a shining example whose work should be upheld and extended.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cranmer's greatest concerns were the maintenance of the royal supremacy and the diffusion of reformed theology and practice. Scholars note that he is best remembered for his contribution to the realms of language and of cultural identity.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> His prose helped to guide the development of the English language, and the <i>Book of Common Prayer</i> is a major contribution to English literature that influenced many lives in the Anglophone world. It has guided Anglican worship for four hundred years.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catholic biographers sometimes depict Cranmer as an unprincipled opportunist, a <a href="/wiki/Nicodemite" title="Nicodemite">Nicodemite</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> and a tool of royal tyranny. For their part, some Protestant biographers appear to overlook the ways or occasions in which Cranmer betrayed his own principles.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Both sides can agree in seeing Cranmer as a committed scholar whose life showed the strengths and weaknesses of a very human and often under-appreciated reformer.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a> commemorates Thomas Cranmer as a Reformation Martyr on 21 March, the anniversary of his death, and he is remembered in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">calendar of saints</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">lesser festival</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">U.S. Episcopal Church liturgical calendar</a> honours him (together with <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Latimer" title="Hugh Latimer">Hugh Latimer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Ridley_(martyr)" title="Nicholas Ridley (martyr)">Nicholas Ridley</a>) with a "day of optional observance" on 16 October.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Citations">Citations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Cranmer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Citations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">

</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirst19342-5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHirst19342_5-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHirst1934">Hirst 1934</a>, p.&#160;2.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown189181-6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown189181_6-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFBrown1891">Brown 1891</a>, p.&#160;81.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-7">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;109. The arms are <i>A chevron between three cranes</i> (Cranmer) and <i>Argent, five fusils in fesse gules each charged with an escallop or</i> (Aslacton).</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;13–15; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;7–15</span>
</li>

</li>

</li>
<li id="cite_note-14"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-14">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFBernard2005">Bernard 2005</a>, p.&#160;506; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;23–33</span>

<li id="cite_note-15"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-15">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch2016">MacCulloch 2016</a>, pp.&#160;xi</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;42. According to MacCulloch, he became convinced of this perhaps as much as two years before his passion for Anne Boleyn.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-17"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-17">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;41–44</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-18"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-18">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;25–33; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;45–51</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-19"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-19">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;54–59. The full title is <i>The Determinations of the most famous and most excellent Universities of Italy and France, that it is unlawful for a man to marry his brother's wife, that the Pope hath no power to dispense therewith</i> and it is likely that Cranmer undertook the translation from Latin to English. Comparing the two language versions, MacCulloch notes that the document reveals the first indications of a change away from his humanist Catholicism towards a more radically reformist stance.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-53">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;137</span>

<li id="cite_note-54"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-54">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;195–206; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;238, 256–274</span>

<li id="cite_note-55"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-55">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHowell1816">Howell 1816</a>, pp.&#160;433–440. According to Howell, several charges were brought against him but the chief one was heresy.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-56"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-56">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;275</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-57"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-57">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;280</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;217–223; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;274–289</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-59"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-59">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;297–308</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-60">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;308–311</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-61"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-61">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;316. It is not known why Henry took so long to react to the charges against Cranmer. MacCulloch notes that it was Henry's nature to brood over the evidence against his archbishop. He also speculates that Cranmer's support of the King's Book made Henry reflect about whether the charges were serious. Another possibility is that in playing the situation out, Henry could observe the behaviour of the leading politicians until he was ready to intervene.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-62"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-62">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;235–238</span>

</li>

</li>
<li id="cite_note-69"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-69">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;265–270; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;365, 369–376</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-70"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-70">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFColeman-Norton1929">Coleman-Norton 1929</a>, p.&#160;279. The epistle was once widely accepted as written by Chrysostom, but is now commonly regarded as a forgery.</span>

<li id="cite_note-76"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-76">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFSpinks1993">Spinks 1993</a>, p.&#160;177</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-77"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-77">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRobinson1998">Robinson 1998</a>, p.&#160;82; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;414–417</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEMills2010189Genocide_and_Ethnocide-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMills2010189Genocide_and_Ethnocide_78-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMills2010">Mills 2010</a>, p.&#160;189, Genocide and Ethnocide.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-79"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-79">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;293–297</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-80"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-80">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;410, 429–437</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-81">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFLoades1993">Loades 1993</a>, p.&#160;160; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;443–447. MacCulloch claims that Paget supported Seymour, but according to Loades it was only Smith who joined with Cranmer. Loades also states that it was likely Cranmer who persuaded Seymour to surrender.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-82"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-82">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;454–459</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEAvis200597–99-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAvis200597–99_83-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFAvis2005">Avis 2005</a>, pp.&#160;97–99.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-84"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-84">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;322–323; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;460–469</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-85"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-85">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;410–411</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-86"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-86">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;308–315; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;469–484</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-87"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-87">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFLoades2004">Loades 2004</a>, pp.&#160;109–111. According to Loades, a felony, a lesser crime than treason in English law, included gathering men unlawfully and plotting the death of a councillor. Seymour admitted to these actions.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-MacCulloch520-88"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-MacCulloch520_88-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-MacCulloch520_88-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;520</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-89"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-89">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;493–500</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-90"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-90">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;501–502</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-91"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-91">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFAyris1993b">Ayris 1993b</a>, pp.&#160;318–321; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;500–502, 518–520, 533</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFBagchiSteinmetz2004">Bagchi &amp; Steinmetz 2004</a>, pp.&#160;158–159</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-93"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-93">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;322–327; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;504–513</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-94"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-94">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFAyris2001">Ayris 2001</a>, pp.&#160;15–17, 29–31</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-95"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-95">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;336–337; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;512, 525–530</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-96"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-96">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;503–504, 524, 536–538</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-97"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-97">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;538–541</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-98"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-98">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHeinze1993">Heinze 1993</a>, pp.&#160;263–264</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-99"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-99">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;547–553</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall2017397-100"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarshall2017397_100-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMarshall2017">Marshall 2017</a>, p.&#160;397.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-101"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-101">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;554–555, 561–562, 572–573 <i>Cf</i>. "he that endureth to the end shall be saved" <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#10:22" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 10:22</a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-102"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-102">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHeinze1993">Heinze 1993</a>, pp.&#160;267–271; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;574–582</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-103"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-103">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHeinze1993">Heinze 1993</a>, pp.&#160;273–276; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;584–599. Heinze and MacCulloch note that Cranmer's recantations can be deduced from two primary sources that had opposite polemical aims, <i>Bishop Cranmer's Recantacyons</i> by an unknown author and <i>Acts and Monuments</i> by <a href="/wiki/John_Foxe" title="John Foxe">John Foxe</a>, also known as <a href="/wiki/Foxe%27s_Book_of_Martyrs" title="Foxe&#39;s Book of Martyrs">Foxe's Book of Martyrs</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-104"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-104">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;597</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-105"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-105">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHeinze1993">Heinze 1993</a>, p.&#160;279; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, p.&#160;603</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-106"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-106">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHeinze1993">Heinze 1993</a>, pp.&#160;277–280; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;600–605. According to Heinze and MacCulloch, an additional corroborating account of Cranmer's execution is found in the letter of a Catholic witness with the initials J. A.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-107"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-107">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;606–608</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-108"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-108">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;148–153; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;361, 481, 609–612</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-109"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-109">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;620–621</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-110"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-110">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFStevenson1993">Stevenson 1993</a>, pp.&#160;189–198; <a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;420–421. Stevenson adds that the marriage vow from the Prayer Book occupies a singular place in the cultural life of the English language.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-111"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-111">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMacCulloch1996">MacCulloch 1996</a>, pp.&#160;630–632</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-112"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-112">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFOverell2008">Overell 2008</a>, p.&#160;207</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-113"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-113">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRidley1962">Ridley 1962</a>, pp.&#160;11–12; <a href="#CITEREFNull2006">Null 2006</a>, pp.&#160;2–17. Null provides an overview of Cranmer scholarship and the different points of view.</span>
</li>
